\mathsf{adom}(F)
that satisfy
\tau
. By construction of the databases
F^{x}/P
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Hom}(F^{x},D^{v},\tau,\Xi^{\prime})|\geq 1

\displaystyle\iff\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}}|\mathsf{Inj}(F^{x}/P,D^{v},\Xi^{\prime}/P)|\geq 1

\displaystyle\iff\bigvee_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}}(|\mathsf{Inj}(F^{x}/P,D^{v},\Xi^{\prime}/P)|\geq 1).

Since GHML
-
is closed under unary disjunction, it thus remains to show how to express
|\mathsf{Inj}(F^{x}/P,D^{v},\Xi^{\prime}/P)|\geq 1
.
Notice that since
F^{x}
is connected,
F^{x}/P
is also connected for all
P
.
To simplify notation, we show how to express
|\mathsf{Inj}(H^{u},D^{v},\xi)|\geq 1
for all connected
H^{u}
and
\xi
that map
\mathsf{adom}(H)
to conjunctions of unary GHML
-
formulas.
In this step we shall exploit the bounded degree of
D^{v}
.

Let
H^{u}
be a connected pointed database,
\xi:\mathsf{adom}(H)\rightarrow L
be a mapping with
L
the set of conjunctions of unary GHML
-
formulas,
and
B\in\mathbb{N}
. We show that there exists a GHML
-
formula
\chi
such that

D\models\chi(v)\iff|\mathsf{Inj}(H^{u},D^{v},\xi)|\geq 1

for all pointed databases
D^{v}
of degree at most
B
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of all partitions of
\mathsf{adom}(H)
.
By Lemma
27
it suffices to find a GHML
-
formula that expresses

\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mu_{P}|\mathsf{Hom}(H^{u}/P,D^{v},\emptyset,\xi/P)|\geq 1.

The property
|\mathsf{Hom}(H^{u}/P,D^{v},\emptyset,\xi/P)|\geq n
, with
P=\{p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
and
u\in p_{0}
,
is expressible by the unary GHML
-
formula

\rho(x_{0})=\exists^{\geq n}\bar{x}\,(\bigwedge_{R(v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{\ell}})\in H^{u}/P}R(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{\ell}})\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\xi_{i}(x_{i})),

where
\bar{x}=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}
and
\xi_{i}=(\xi/P)(p_{i})
.
If each formula
\xi(v)
is connected, then the above formula is also connected, since
H^{u}/P
is a connected database and each
\xi/P
is connected.

Since
H^{u}
is connected and the degree of all relevant databases
D^{v}
is bounded by
B
, the number of homomorphisms in
\mathsf{Hom}(H^{u}/P,D^{v},\emptyset,\xi/P)
is also bounded.
Therefore, Property (
B.2
) can be expressed as a finite Boolean combination of unary GHML
-
formulas, which itself is again a GHML
-
formula.
This also preserves connectedness.

We now consider the case where
\psi(x)=\exists^{\geq k_{1}}x_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{n}}x_{n}\,\varphi(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is not top-level connected.
Let
k=\mathsf{max}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
then

\psi(x)\equiv\exists^{\geq k}y\,\top\land\exists^{\geq k_{1}}x_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{n}}x_{n}\,\varphi(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).

It is now safe to remove
\exists^{\geq k_{i}}x_{i}
if
x_{i}
does not occur in
\varphi
.
Fix some
B\in\mathbb{N}
and restrict to databases of degree at most
B
.
Using well-known equivalences of first-order logic, and the previous observation that unused quantifications can be removed, we can rewrite
\psi
into a conjunction of existentially quantified formulas, each of which is top-level connected; exactly one
of these contains the free variable
x
. More precisely,
\psi
can be rewritten into a conjunction of

1.

a formula
\exists^{\geq\ell_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq\ell_{m}}y_{m}\,\varphi^{\prime}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
that is top-level connected;

2.

formulas
\exists^{\geq\ell_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq\ell_{m}}y_{m}\,\varphi^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})

that are all top-level connected;

3.

the formula
\exists^{\geq k}y\,\top

such that, for distinct formulas, the sets of variables quantified in the outermost quantifier block are disjoint. We have already described how to transform the formula in Point 1 into a GHML
-
formula that is equivalent over all databases of degree at most
B
.
Each formula
\xi
in Point 2 can be treated as follows. We first translate

\exists^{\geq\ell_{2}}y_{2}\cdots\exists^{\geq\ell_{m}}y_{m}\,\varphi^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})

into a GHML
-
formula
\psi(y_{1})
that is equivalent over all databases of degree at most
B
, as above. Then,
\exists^{\geq\ell_{1}}y_{1}\,\psi^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\xi
over all databases of degree at most
B
where
\psi^{\prime}
is obtained from
\psi
by adding an atom
\mathsf{true}(z)

for a fresh variable
z
, to ensure that
there is a free variable.
The third formula is already in GHML
-
.
Since GHML
-
is closed under conjunction, this finishes the proof.
∎

We now prove Point 3 of Theorem
7
, starting with the direction from GHML to
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs.
The translation does
not depend on connectedness, but preserves it. By Lemma
29
, we may start from GHML
-
in place of GHML.
Note that, while the subsequent translation does not depend on bounded degree databases, Lemma
29
does.

Lemma 30
.

Every (connected) GHML
-
formula is equivalent to a (connected) simple
\mathsf{sum}
-DHN.

Proof.

The proof follows the same strategy as that of Lemma
24
. The cases where a subformula
\varphi_{i}
is a negation or of the form
\varphi_{i_{1}}\vee\varphi_{i_{2}}
can
be handled in exactly the same way as in the proof of Lemma
24
. In fact, all homomorphism queries
(F^{v},\mu,\mathsf{max})
used in these cases satisfy
{\mathsf{adom}(F)=\{v\}}
and therefore
for any
G^{\bullet}
the set
\mathsf{Hom}(F^{v},G^{\bullet})
has cardinality at most one. As a consequence,

\mathsf{eval}((F^{\bullet},\mu,\mathsf{sum}),(G^{\bullet},\lambda))=\mathsf{eval}((F^{\bullet},\mu,\mathsf{max}),(G^{\bullet},\lambda))
.

We treat the remaining case where
\varphi_{i}
takes the form

\exists^{\geq n}\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
.
It is equivalent to

\exists^{\geq n}\,\Big(\psi_{0}\land\bigwedge_{z\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}}\psi_{z}\Big),

where
\psi_{0}
is a conjunction of relational atoms and each
\psi_{z}
is a conjunction of GHML
-
formulas with the free variable
z
.
We again use two homomorphism queries, one for evaluating the conjuncts in
\psi
and one for copying the previous embedding.

The first homomorphism query
(F_{1}^{x},\mu_{1},\mathsf{sum})
of output dimension
1
is defined as follows:

•

F_{1}
contains as values the variable
x
and all variables in
\bar{y}
,

•

F_{1}
contains the fact
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
for every atom
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
in
\psi_{0}
,

•

\mu_{1}
assigns to each value
z\in\mathsf{adom}(F_{1}^{x})
the transformation function that maps every tuple
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
to the tuple
(1)
if the
j
-th position of
\bar{x}
is
1
for all conjuncts
\varphi_{j}(z)
in
\psi_{z}
, and the tuple
(0)
otherwise.

Notice that
F_{1}^{x}
is connected if
\varphi_{i}
is connected.

The second query
(F_{2}^{\bullet},\mu_{2},\mathsf{sum})
of output dimension
k
is defined as follows:

•

F_{2}^{\bullet}
is the graph with exactly one value, without any facts,

•

if
i=1
,
\mu_{2}
returns the
k
-dimensional vector with all entries set to
0
, and

•

if
i>1
,
\mu_{2}
is the identity function.

The combination function
\mathsf{com}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}

•

copies all entries except the first and the
i+1
-st,

•

discards the
i+1
-st entry, and

•

subtracts
n-1
from the first entry, copies the result to the
i+1
-th entry, and applies
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
.

It is easy to verify that
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},
and
\mathsf{com}_{i}
are affine with ReLU activation.
It is also easy to verify that the homomorphism number regarding
(F_{1}^{x},\mu_{1},\mathsf{sum})
returns the number of tuples such that
\psi(x,\bar{y})
is satisfied.
Thus, the combination function returns
1
in the
i
-th entry if and only if this number is at least
n
.
∎

We next translate connected DHNs to connected GHML
-
, assuming bounded degree databases. Under the assumption of bounded degree, it is possible to prove an analogue of Lemma
25

also in the case of sum aggregation.
This is in fact implicit in the proof
of the following result.

Lemma 31
.

Let
B\geq 0
.
On databases of degree at most
B
, every connected DHN is equivalent to a connected GHML
-
formula.

Proof.

Let
B\geq 0
and let
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
be a connected DHN. Further let
n
be the maximum size of databases in homomorphism queries in
\cal N
, measured in the number of values. Every pointed database
G^{v}
induces a pointed database of diameter
kn
, simply by taking the restriction of
G^{v}
to the values
u
that are in distance of at most
kn
from
v
.

Take two pointed databases
G_{1}^{v_{1}}
and
G_{2}^{v_{2}}
.
It follows directly from the definition of connected DHNs that if
G_{1}^{v_{1}}
and
G_{2}^{v_{2}}
induce the
same database of diameter
kn
, then

\lambda^{k}_{\mathcal{N},G_{1}}(v_{1})=\lambda^{k}_{\mathcal{N},G_{2}}(v_{2})
and thus

\mathcal{N}
accepts both of these databases
or neither of them.

If a pointed database
G^{\bullet}
has diameter at most
kn
and degree at most
B
, then the number of values in
G^{\bullet}
is at most
(B+1)^{kn+1}-1
, and thus the number of such databases is bounded as well.

It thus suffices to find a connected GHML
-
formula
\varphi(x)
such that
for every pointed database
G^{v}
of degree at most
B
,

G\models\varphi(v)
if and only if

G^{v}
induces a pointed database
F^{v}
of diameter
kn
(and also of degree at most
B
) such that
\cal N

accepts
F^{v}
. Since there are only finitely many such
F^{v}
(up to isomorphism), it suffices to find for each of them
a GHML
-
formula
\psi_{F^{v}}
such that
G\models\psi_{F^{v}}(v)
if and only
if
G^{v}
induces exactly
F^{v}
and then take the disjunction.

To construct the formulas
\psi_{F^{v}}
, in turn, we may use Lovász’ homomorphism count theorem.
We can consider all pointed databases
H^{w}
with
|\mathsf{adom}(H)|\leq|\mathsf{adom}(F)|
and count
the exact number of homomorphism from

H^{w}
using Boolean combinations of
formulas of the form
\exists^{\geq k}\bar{y}\,\psi(w,\bar{y})
where
\psi

is the conjunction that has one atom
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
for every fact
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\in H
. Based on Theorem
1
, we can then find a Boolean combination
\psi_{F^{v}}
that is as required.
∎

We remark that in the proof of the previous lemma, the constructed GHML
-
formula does not nest the operators
\exists^{\geq k}\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
.
This remark and Lemma
30
show that, on bounded degree databases, every connected GHML
-
formula is equivalent to a connected GHML
-
formula that does not nest these particular operators.

We next show that the characterizations in Point 3 of Theorem
7
cannot be lifted to databases of unbounded degree, even if we restrict the
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs to properties definable in first-order logic.
See
8

To prove this statement, we will first show that local transitivity can be expressed by
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs.
To show that GHML cannot express this property, we will then introduce GHML games.
They are defined analogously to Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé games, which are a useful tool to prove which properties are (in-)expressible in first-order logic.

Lemma 32
.

There is a connected simple
\mathsf{sum}
-DHN over the graph schema that accepts a
pointed database
D^{v}
if and only if
v
is locally transitive in
D
.

Proof.

For this, we use two homomorphism queries
(F^{v_{1}},\mu,\mathsf{sum})
and

(F^{\prime v_{1}^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime},\mathsf{sum})
, where
{F=\{(v_{1},v_{2}),(v_{2},v_{3})\}}
and

\mu_{v_{i}}
is the constant
1
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,
F^{\prime}=\{(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2}),(v^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{3}),(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{3})\}
, and
\mu^{\prime}_{v_{i}}
is the constant
1
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Observe that by choice of
\mu
as well as the
\mathsf{sum}
,

\mathsf{eval}((F^{v_{1}},\mu,\mathsf{sum}),(D^{v},\lambda))=|\mathsf{Hom}(F^{v_{1}},D^{v})|
and similarly

\mathsf{eval}((F^{\prime v_{1}^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime},\mathsf{sum}),(D^{v},\lambda))=|\mathsf{Hom}(F^{\prime v_{1}^{\prime}},D^{v})|
.
Clearly,
v
is locally transitive in
D
if and only if
|\mathsf{Hom}(F^{v_{1}},D^{v})|=|\mathsf{Hom}(F^{\prime v_{1}^{\prime}},D^{v})|
.
The desired DHN uses two layers.
The first one computes

x_{1}=\mathsf{eval}((F^{v_{1}},\mu,\mathsf{sum}),(D^{v},\lambda))
and
x_{2}=\mathsf{eval}((F^{\prime v_{1}^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime},\mathsf{sum}),(D^{v},\lambda))
, and then
uses the combination function to compute
y_{1}=\textup{ReLU}^{*}(x_{2}-x_{1})
and
y_{2}=\textup{ReLU}^{*}(x_{1}-x_{2})
.
The second layer computes
z=\textup{ReLU}^{*}(1-y_{1}-y_{2})
,
which is
1
iff
x_{1}=x_{2}
.
The final classification function accepts
D^{v}
if
z=1
and rejects otherwise.
∎

To show
Proposition
8
it remains to prove that local
transitivity cannot be expressed by a GHML formula.

The
depth
of a GHML formula
\varphi
is defined as the number of times
subformulas of the form

\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
are nested in
\varphi
.
This means that, there are no GHML formulas of depth
0
, and the formula
\exists^{\geq 2}yR(x,y)
has depth
1
.
The
width
of a GHML formula
\varphi
is the smallest number
n
such that

n\geq m

for all subformulas of the form

\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
.
The
counting bound
of a GHML formula
\varphi
is the smallest number
c

such that
c\geq k_{i}
for all subformulas of the form

\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
and
all
i
with
1\leq i\leq m
.

A homomorphism from a (non-pointed) database
F
to a (non-pointed) database
D

is defined as expected. For a database
D
and a set
X\subseteq\mathsf{adom}(D)
, we denote with
D|X
the restriction of
D
to the values
in
X
.

Definition 33

(GHML game)
.

Let
d,n,c\geq 1
. The
d
-round GHML game of width
n
with
counting bound
c
is played between two players,
Spoiler
and

Duplicator
, on two databases
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.
The positions of the game are pairs
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathsf{adom}(D_{1})\times\mathsf{adom}(D_{2})
.

In each of the
d
rounds,
Spoiler
first picks an
s\in\{1,2\}
.
The players then repeat the following steps
n
times to construct tuples
\bar{u}_{1}\in\mathsf{adom}(D_{1})^{n}
and
\bar{u}_{2}\in\mathsf{adom}(D_{2})^{n}
.

•

Spoiler
picks distinct values
w_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{s}^{(k)}\in\mathsf{adom}(D_{s})
where
k
is at most
c
.

•

Duplicator
responds with distinct values
w_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{3-s}^{(k)}\in\mathsf{adom}(D_{3-s})
.
If there are less than
k
values in
D_{3-s}
,
Spoiler
wins immediately.

•

Spoiler
picks an index
j
and appends
w_{1}^{(j)}
and
w_{2}^{(j)}
to
\bar{u}_{1}
and
\bar{u}_{2}
respectively.

After the tuples are completed, it is checked whether there exists a homomorphism
h
from
D_{s}|\{v_{s},u_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{s}^{(n)}\}
to
D_{3-s}|\{v_{3-s},u_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{3-s}^{(n)}\}

that satisfies
h(v_{s})=v_{3-s}
and
{h(u_{s}^{(i)})=u_{3-s}^{(i)}}
.
If this is not the case, Spoiler wins immediately.
Otherwise,
Spoiler
picks an index
i
. The pair
(u_{1}^{(i)},u_{2}^{(i)})
becomes the position for the next round.

Duplicator
wins if
Spoiler
does not win after
\ell
rounds.

Lemma 34
.

For all
d\geq 1
and
n,c\geq 0
and all pointed databases
D_{1}^{v_{1}}
,

D_{2}^{v_{2}}
the following holds:

If
Duplicator
has a winning strategy in the
d
-round GHML
game of width
n
with counting bound
c
on
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
from initial
position
(v_{1},v_{2})
, then for all GHML formulas
\varphi(x)
of depth
at most
\ell
, width at most
n

and counting bound at most
c
,

D_{1}\models\varphi(v_{1})\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad D_{2}\models\varphi(v_{2}).

Proof.

We show the lemma by induction on
d
. In the induction start
d=1
and we have to show this result for formulas
\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
,
where each
\psi_{i}
is a conjunction of relational atoms.
Since this is only a special case of the induction step, where the
\psi_{i}
can also contain unary formulas,
we refer to the induction step.

In the induction step, let
d>1
and assume that the statement holds for
all
d^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}<d
.
Further, assume that
Duplicator
has a winning strategy in the

d
-round game of width
n
with counting bound
c
from the position
(v_{1},v_{2})
.
Let
\varphi(x)
be a GHML formula of depth
d
, width at most
n

and counting bound
c
.
To show the statement, it suffices to show that for all subformulas of
\varphi
of the form

\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
with
m\leq n
and
k_{i}\leq c

it holds that

\displaystyle D_{1}\models\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{1},y_{1},\dots,y_{m})

if and only if

\displaystyle D_{2}\models\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{2},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}).

Now assume that there is such a subformula with

D_{s}\models\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{1},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}).

If
Spoiler
plays in
D_{s}
,
Duplicator
must be able to respond in

D_{3-s}
appropriately.
In particular, this means that when
Spoiler
picks
k_{1}
distinct values

w_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{s}^{(k_{1})}
that satisfy
\exists^{\geq k_{2}}y_{2}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{s},w_{s}^{(i)},y_{2},\ldots,y_{m})
, then
Duplicator
must be able to respond
with
k_{1}
distinct values
w_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{3-s}^{(k_{1})}
of which
Spoiler
can pick any to continue.
After
m
steps,
Spoiler
can thus arrive at tuples
\bar{u}_{1}
and
\bar{u}_{2}

such that

D_{s}\models\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{s},\bar{u}_{s}).

Hence, there exists an
1\leq i\leq\ell

such that

D_{s}\models\psi_{i}(v_{s},\bar{u}_{s}).

As
Duplicator
has a winning strategy for the
d
-round game, it must
be the case that (i)
h
with
h(v_{s})=v_{3-s}
and
h(u_{s}^{(i)})=u_{3-s}^{(i)}
is a homomorphism from
D_{s}|\{v_{s},u_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{s}^{(m)}\}
to
D_{3-s}|\{v_{3-s},u_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{3-s}^{(n)}\}
, and
(ii) that for any
i
that
Spoiler
picks,
Duplicator
has a
winning strategy for the
(d-1)
-round game in the pair
(u_{1}^{(i)},u_{2}^{(i)})
.
Point (i) implies that
v_{3-s}
and
\bar{u}_{3-s}
satisfy all relational atoms in
\psi_{i}
.
Point (ii) implies, via the induction hypothesis, that
v_{3-s}
and
\bar{u}_{3-s}
satisfy
all unary GHML formulas in
\psi_{i}
. Together it
follows that

D_{3-s}\models\psi_{i}(v_{3-s},\bar{u}_{3-s})\quad\text{ and }\quad D_{3-s}\models\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{3-s},\bar{u}_{3-s}).

As
Duplicator
must be able to respond to all choices of
Spoiler
,
it also follows that

D_{3-s}\models\exists^{\geq k_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq k_{m}}y_{m}\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\psi_{i}(v_{3-s},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}).\qed

Using GHML games, we now proceed to show that local transitivity is not
expressible in GHML.

Lemma 35
.

For all
d,n,c\geq 0
, there exist databases
D_{1}^{v_{1}}
,
D_{2}^{v_{2}}

over the graph schema such that
D_{1}^{v_{1}}
is locally transitive,

D_{2}^{v_{2}}
is not locally transitive and

Duplicator
has a winning strategy in the
d
-round GHML game
of width
n
and counting bound
c
starting in position
(v_{1},v_{2})
.

Proof.

To show the lemma, we use the following families
F_{1}^{m}
,
F_{2}^{m}
of databases
over the graph schema.
For
m\geq 0
, set

F_{1}^{m}=\{E(v_{a},v_{b})\mid 1\leq a<b\leq 2m+3\}

and
F_{2}^{m}=F_{1}^{m}\setminus\{E(m+1,m+3)\}
. Clearly,
v_{m+1}
in
F_{1}^{m}
is locally
transitive and
v_{m+1}
in
F_{2}^{m}
is not locally transitive.
The structure of
F_{1}^{m}
and
F_{2}^{m}
is visualized in Figure
1
.
We define the function
\ind(v_{i})=i
.

v_{1}
\cdots
v_{m+1}
v_{m+2}
v_{m+3}
\cdots
v_{2m+3}

Figure 1:
Structure of the databases
F_{1}^{m}
and
F_{2}^{m}
with
F_{1}^{m}\setminus F_{2}^{m}
highlighted in red.

We now show the following claim which implies the lemma.

Claim.

For all
d,n,c\geq 0
,
if
m\geq d(c\,2^{n+1}-c+1)
, then
Duplicator
has a winning
strategy in the
d
-round GHML game of width
n
and counting bound
c
on
F_{1}^{m}
and
F_{2}^{m}

for any starting position
(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
\ind(x_{1})=\ind(x_{2})
or

d(c\,2^{n+1}-c+1)+1\leq\ind(x_{1}),\ind(x_{2})\leq 2m+3-d(c\,2^{n+1}-c+1)

We start by describing the idea behind the constant
c\,2^{n+1}-c+1
.
Assume that two integers
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
y-x\geq c\,2^{n+1}-c+1
.
Then there exist
c
integers
{z_{1}=x+c\,2^{n}-c+1},\ldots,{z_{c}=x+c\,2^{n}}
that satisfy

z_{i}-x\geq c\,2^{n}-c+1

and

y-z_{i}\geq c\,2^{n+1}-c+1+x-z_{i}\geq c\,2^{n}-c+1.

Proof of the claim.

We prove that
Duplicator
has a winning strategy where they never choose
v_{m+1}
and
v_{m+3}
in
F_{3-s}^{m}
, with the exception that they may choose the starting position.
Within one round, they have to ensure that, at any time, there is enough space between chosen vertices in
F_{3-s}^{m}
and that
they respond with vertices that have the same index if
Spoiler
chooses vertices near one of the ends.
We show that the Condition (
*_{d}
) gives
Duplicator
enough freedom, without allowing
Spoiler
to win.

For readability, set
N_{k}=c\,2^{n+1-k}-c+1
and
M=2m+3
.
We show the claim by induction on
d
.
In the induction start,
d=0
and the statement holds trivially.

Now assume that the claim holds for
d-1
, and that an
d
-round
GHML game of width
n
and counting bound
c

starts in position
(x_{1},x_{2})
for some
x_{1},x_{2}
that satisfy
\ind(x_{1})=\ind(x_{2})
or (
*_{d}
).

Let
s\in\{1,2\}
be the choice of
Spoiler
, that is, they play in
F_{s}^{m}
and

Duplicator
responds with vertices in
F_{3-s}^{m}
.

Duplicator
has to deal with the following problems:
If
\ind(x_{1})<\ind(x_{2})
, then there are fewer vertices right of
x_{2}
than there are vertices right of
x_{1}
,
and additionally,
Duplicator
may have to shift the indices of their response to avoid choosing
v_{m+1}
or
v_{m+3}
.

For this, let
p_{1}=(d-1)N_{0}+1
and
p_{2}=M-(d-1)N_{0}
.

Duplicator
divides the graphs into five sections. We write them as intervals of indices.

•

Two sections at the ends of the graphs defined by
{A=[1,p_{1}-1]\cup[p_{2}+1,M]}
.

•

Two sections with the intervals
B_{1}=[p_{1},p_{1}+N_{0}-1]
and
B_{2}=[p_{2}-N_{0}+1,p_{2}]
.

•

The center defined by the interval
{C=[p_{1}+N_{0},p_{2}-N_{0}]}
.

Duplicator
will play as follows:
If
Spoiler
chooses a vertex in
A
,
Duplicator
will respond with the same vertex in
F_{3-s}
.
Otherwise,
Duplicator
will choose a vertex in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
which avoids choosing
v_{m+1}
and
v_{m+3}
which are in section
C
.

Duplicator
may choose
x_{3-s}
even if it is in
C
.
The starting positions satisfy
x_{1}\in C
iff
x_{2}\in C
, and if
x_{1},x_{2}\in A\cup B_{1}\cup B_{2}
, then by assumption
\ind(x_{1})=\ind(x_{2})
.
This strategy ensures
that each selected pair
(u_{1},u_{2})
will satisfy
\ind(u_{1})=\ind(u_{2})
or (
*_{d-1}
),
since the bounds of these zones are equal to the bounds of (
*_{d-1}
).
By induction hypothesis,
Duplicator
then has a winning strategy in the
d-1
-round GHML game with width
n
and counting bound
c
.

Thus, we only have to show that
Duplicator
can choose vertices according to this strategy without loosing this round.
Within this round, both players will construct tuples
\bar{u}_{1}
and
\bar{u}_{2}
with
n
elements.
We show by induction on
n
that
Duplicator
can choose vertices such that these tuples satisfy the following conditions.

1.

Duplicator
responds with vertices according to the sections above.
They don’t choose vertices in
C
except
x_{3-s}
.

2.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2}),(u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime})
be two played pairs which are in the interval
[p_{1},p_{2}]
. Then

\ind(u_{s})\sim\ind(u_{s}^{\prime})\text{ iff }\ind(u_{3-s})\sim\ind(u_{3-s}^{\prime})\text{ for all }\sim\,\in\{<,=,>\}.

3.

If
x_{1},x_{2}\not\in B_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
:
After selecting
k
vertices, let
(u_{1},u_{2}),(u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime})
be two played pairs such that
u_{3-s},u_{3-s}^{\prime}\in B_{i}
.
Then they satisfy:

•

if
\ind(u_{3-s})\neq\ind(u_{3-s}^{\prime})
then
|\ind(u_{3-s})-\ind(u_{3-s}^{\prime})|\geq N_{k}
, and

•

|\ind(u_{3-s})-p_{i}|\geq N_{k}
and
|p_{i}+N_{0}-1-\ind(u_{3-s})|\geq N_{k}
.

4.

If
x_{1},x_{2}\in B_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
:
Let
(u_{s},u_{3-s})
be a played pair such that
\ind(u_{s})\in B_{i}
. Then
\ind(u_{s})=\ind(u_{3-s})
.

These conditions are obviously satisfied for
k=0
.

Now let
\bar{u}_{1}
and
\bar{u}_{2}
be tuples with
k
elements for some
0\leq k<n
.
The game proceeds with
Spoiler
choosing vertices
w_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{s}^{(c^{\prime})}
, where
c^{\prime}
is at most
c
.

Duplicator
now has to choose
c^{\prime}
distinct elements
w_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,w_{3-s}^{(c^{\prime})}
such that
\bar{u}_{1},w_{1}^{(i)},\bar{u}_{2}
, and
w_{2}^{(i)}
satisfy the conditions above for each
i
.

Fix some
w_{s}^{(i)}
.
If
w_{s}^{(i)}\in A
or
x_{1},x_{2},w_{s}^{(i)}\in B_{j}
then
Duplicator
chooses the vertex with index
\ind(w_{s}^{(i)})
in
F_{3-s}^{m}
.
If
w_{s}^{(i)}=x_{s}
then
Duplicator
chooses
w_{3-s}^{(i)}=x_{3-s}
.
We next clarify how
Duplicator
chooses their response in the other cases, that is
w_{s}^{(i)}\in C
or
w_{s}^{(i)}\in B_{j}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\not\in B_{j}
.

Duplicator
then chooses a
j\in\{1,2\}
such that their response must be in
B_{j}
to satisfy Condition 2.
If
x_{1},x_{2}\in B_{3-j}
then
Duplicator
can choose
B_{j}
.
They now find the interval
[e_{1},e_{2}]
within
B_{j}
in which their response has to be to satisfy Condition 2,
such that both,
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are the index of some played vertex or one of the limits of
B_{j}
.
If
e_{1}=e_{2}
, then
w_{s}^{(i)}
was chosen by
Spoiler
before and is thus in
\bar{u}_{s}
and
Duplicator
can respond with the corresponding vertex in
\bar{u}_{3-s}
.
Otherwise, observe that by the Condition 3,
e_{2}-e_{1}\geq N_{k}
.
Therefore, there exist at least
c
vertices
v_{d}
such that
d
satisfies
e_{2}-d\geq N_{k+1}
and
d-e_{1}\geq N_{k+1}
.

Duplicator
then chooses one of these elements.
Because there are always
c
of these elements,
Duplicator
can always choose a fresh vertex for each
w_{s}^{(i)}
.

After that,
Spoiler
chooses one
w_{s}^{(i)}
and the corresponding response and appends these vertices to
\bar{u}_{1}
and
\bar{u}_{2}
, which now contain
k+1
vertices.
It is easy to see that the four conditions above are still satisfied.

This strategy ensures that in the final tuple
\bar{u}_{3-s}
the order of the values
is the same as in the tuple
\bar{u}_{s}
.
And additionally by choice of the intervals,
it is never the case that
Duplicator
plays
v_{m+1}
and
v_{m+3}
at the same time.
Thus,
h
with
h(x_{s})=x_{3-s}
and
h(u_{s}^{(i)})=u_{3-s}^{(i)}
is a homomorphism from
F_{s}^{m}|\{v_{s},u_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{s}^{(n)}\}
to
F_{3-s}^{m}|\{v_{3-s},u_{3-s}^{(1)},\ldots,u_{3-s}^{(n)}\}
, as required.
Hence,
Duplicator
does not lose this round.
As described before, by induction hypothesis,
Duplicator
thus has a winning strategy in the
d
-round GHML game with width
n
and counting bound
c
.
∎

Lemma
35
and Lemma
34

now together imply that no GHML formula can
express local transitivity.
This completes the proof of Proposition
8
.

B.3
Max and Sum Aggregation: Static Analysis

We now use the logical equivalences to find upper and lower bounds for
the complexity of the emptiness and subsumption problems for DHNs.
We show each point of Theorem
9
.

For
\mathsf{max}
-DHNs, we can use known complexity results for UNFO.

Theorem 36
.

The emptiness problem and the subsumption problem for
\mathsf{max}
-DHNs are decidable.

Proof.

Decidability of the emptiness problem is an immediate consequence of the facts that (i) the translations in the proof of Point 1 of Theorem
7

are effective, (ii) the satisfiability problem for UNFO is decidable, and (iii) UNFO has the finite model property
[
31
]
.
These facts also imply decidability of the subsumption problem. Given DHNs
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
, we construct
equivalent UNFO sentences
\varphi_{1}

and
\varphi_{2}
and decide whether

\varphi_{1}\wedge\neg\varphi_{2}

is unsatisfiable (equivalently:
finitely unsatisfiable).
∎

On databases of bounded degree and for connected DHNs, decidability follows from a counting argument.
Lower bounds for connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs can again be obtained from complexity results for the corresponding logics.

Theorem 37
.

Let
B\geq 4
.
On databases of degree at most
B
, the emptiness problem and the subsumption problem for connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs are decidable and
coNExpTime
-complete. The lower bounds already hold on vertex-labeled directed graphs.

Proof (sketch).

We first describe an algorithm that decides the non-emptiness problem of
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs in
NExpTime
.
This algorithm receives as input
a
\mathsf{sum}
-DHN
\mathcal{N}
over some schema
\mathbf{S}
. Whether we consider the schema
\mathbf{S}
as fixed or part of the input makes no difference for the subsequent proof.
Let
\ell
be the number of layers of
\mathcal{N}
and let
k
be the size of the largest database
F^{\bullet}
that occurs in some homomorphism query of
\mathcal{N}
.
For each database
D^{v}
, the output of
\mathcal{N}
on
D^{v}
only depends on the
\ell k
neighborhood of
v
.
Thus, there exists a database that
\mathcal{N}
accepts iff there exists a database of diameter at most
\ell k
that
\mathcal{N}
accepts.
Such databases have at most
B^{\ell k}
values and
B^{\ell kr}
facts, where
r
is the sum of the arities of the relation symbols in
\mathbf{S}
.
Hence, these databases have a size at most single exponential in
\ell,k,r
which are all bounded from above by the input length.
Thus, a
NExpTime
algorithm can guess a database of this size and then check in polynomial time (in the size of the database) whether it is accepted by
\mathcal{N}
.

To show that the non-emptiness problem of connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs is
NExpTime
-hard, we use the translation of GHML
-
formulas to
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs provided in the proof of Lemma
30
, which is polynomial.
We thus have to show that finite satisfiability in connected GHML
-

is
NExpTime
-hard.
To start with, note that connected GHML
-
contains modal logic (ML) as
a proper fragment and (finite) satisfiability of uni-modal ML formulas is
PSpace
-hard. The standard proof by reduction from quantified Boolean formulas even yields this result on directed graphs of degree at most 3. Thus the non-emptiness problem for
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs is at least

PSpace
-hard, even on vertex-labeled directed graphs of degree at most 3. However, it follows from the results in
[
25
]
that finite satisfiability in connected HML (and thus also in connected GHML
-
) is even
NExpTime
-hard, already on vertex-labeled directed graphs of degree at most 4.
More precisely, it is proved in

[
25
]
that for a given uni-modal ML formula
\varphi

and unary connected conjunctive query
q(x)
,
it is
coNExpTime
-hard to decide whether
\varphi
implies
q
in the sense that for all (potentially infinite) pointed vertex-labeled directed graphs
(G,v)
,
(G,v)\models\varphi
implies
(G,v)\models q(x)
.
†
†

Note that
[
25
]
uses a different language. The stated result is Theorem 1 of that article.

It is implicit in the proofs of
[
25
]
that this problem coincides with the restricted version where only graphs
G
are considered that are finite and of degree at most 4. Now, it is easy to see that
\varphi
(finitely) implies
q
if and only if
the HML formula
\varphi\wedge\neg q
is not finitely satisfiable. Note that here we view the unary CQ
q
as a HML formula of the form
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
.
Clearly,
NExpTime
-hardness of finite satisfiability in connected HML follows.

The subsumption problem can be solved as follows:
\mathcal{N}_{1}
does not subsume
\mathcal{N}_{2}
iff there exists a database that is accepted by
\mathcal{N}_{2}
but not by
\mathcal{N}_{1}
.
The size of such a database can be bounded as in the non-emptiness case.
Thus, the subsumption problem can be solved in
coNExpTime
.
Subsumption for GHML
-
is
coNExpTime
-hard since a GHML
-
formula
\varphi
is satisfiable iff
\bot
does not subsume
\varphi
.
By using the translation in the proof of Lemma
30
, we can reduce subsumption of GHML
-
formulas to subsumption of
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs.
Thus, the subsumption problem of
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs is
coNExpTime
-complete.
∎

The following is Point 3 of Theorem
9
.

Theorem 38
.

The emptiness problem and the subsumption problem for connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs are undecidable, already for the graph schema.
The same is true for (not necessarily connected)
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs on graphs of any bounded degree
B\geq 5
.

Proof.

We start with
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs that are not necessarily connected and graphs of bounded or unbounded degree and later sketch the modification necessary for connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs on graphs of unbounded degree.
By Lemma
30
, it suffices to prove
that the finite satisfiability of GHML
-
formulas is undecidable, both on unrestricted graphs and on graphs of bounded degree.
We remark that finite satisfiability in UNFOC is known to be undecidable
on unrestricted graphs, a proof is implicit in the proof of Theorem 7.1 of
[
31
]
. But since GHML
-
is less expressive than UNFOC and we are also interested in graphs of bounded
degree,
we sketch a direct proof by reduction of the tiling problem that
asks for tiling a finite rectangle using restricted sets of tiles at the borders. In addition to the edge relation
E
of graphs, we use unary relation symbols and later argue how these can be removed.

A
tiling system
takes the form
S=(T,L,R,U,D,H,V)

where
T
is a finite set of tile types,
L,R,U,D\subseteq T

are sets of tiles to be used at the left, right, upper, and lower borders,
and
H,V\subseteq T^{2}
are the
horizontal and vertical matching conditions. A
solution
for
S
is a triple
(n,m,f)
with
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
f:\{1,\dots,n\}\times\{1,\dots,m\}\rightarrow T

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

for
1\leq i<n
and
1\leq j\leq m
:
(f(i,j),f(i+1,j))\in H
;

2.

for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j<m
:
(f(i,j),f(i,j+1))\in V
;

3.

for
1\leq j\leq m
:
f(1,j)\in L
and
f(n,j)\in R
;

4.

for
1\leq i\leq n
:
f(i,1)\in D
and
f(i,m)\in U
.

It is shown in
[
6
]
that the problem of satisfiable tiling systems is undecidable.

We show how to construct
a GHML
-
formula
\varphi_{S}
such that

(a)

if there is a solution for
S
, then
\varphi_{S}
is finitely satisfiable in a graph of degree at most 4 and

(b)

if
\varphi_{S}
is finitely satisfiable, then there is a solution for
S
.

We simulate two binary relations
that form a grid, a horizontal and a vertical one. Each edge of these relations is simulated via two consecutive edges and a propositional letter
H
or
V

satisfied at the middle point.
We also introduce a propositional
letter
P_{T}
for every tile type
t\in T
, a letter
G
to identify vertices on the grid (as opposed to middle vertices on simulated edges), and
letters
B^{\leftarrow},B^{\rightarrow},B^{\downarrow},B^{\uparrow}
to identify the borders.
We set

\varphi_{S}=\exists\alpha\,(B^{\leftarrow}(\alpha)\wedge B^{\downarrow}(\alpha)\wedge G(\alpha))\wedge\forall x\,\vartheta(x)

where
\vartheta
is a conjunction that consists of the following conjuncts:

1.

The vertices at the borders are exactly the vertices that have no corresponding neighbor

\displaystyle(G(x)\land\neg B^{\rightarrow}(x))\leftrightarrow\exists y_{1}y_{2}\,(E(x,y_{1})\land H(y_{1})\land E(y_{1},y_{2})\land G(y_{2}))

\displaystyle\land\,

\displaystyle(G(x)\land\neg B^{\leftarrow}(x))\leftrightarrow\exists y_{1}y_{2}\,(E(y_{1},x)\land H(y_{1})\land E(y_{2},y_{1})\land G(y_{2}))

\displaystyle\land\,

\displaystyle(G(x)\land\neg B^{\uparrow}(x))\leftrightarrow\exists y_{1}y_{2}\,(E(x,y_{1})\land V(y_{1})\land E(y_{1},y_{2})\land G(y_{2}))

\displaystyle\land\,

\displaystyle(G(x)\land\neg B^{\downarrow}(x))\leftrightarrow\exists y_{1}y_{2}\,(E(y_{1},x)\land V(y_{1})\land E(y_{2},y_{1})\land G(y_{2}))

2.

Every grid vertex, that is not on the upper or right border is the origin of a grid cell:

\displaystyle(G(x)\wedge\neg B^{\uparrow}(x)\wedge\neg B^{\rightarrow}(x))\qquad

\displaystyle\rightarrow\exists y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}u\,\big(

\displaystyle E(x,y_{1})\wedge V(y_{1})\wedge E(y_{1},y_{2})\wedge G(y_{2})

\displaystyle\land\,E(x,z_{1})\wedge H(z_{1})\wedge E(z_{1},z_{2})\wedge G(z_{2})

\displaystyle\land\,E(y_{2},y_{3})\wedge H(y_{3})\wedge E(y_{3},u)

\displaystyle\land\,E(z_{2},z_{3})\wedge V(z_{3})\wedge E(z_{3},u)\wedge G(u)

\displaystyle\big)

3.

The horizontal and vertical grid relations are functional:

\displaystyle G(x)\rightarrow

\displaystyle\big(\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,(E(x,y)\wedge V(y))\wedge\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,(E(y,x)\wedge V(y))

\displaystyle\phantom{\big(}\land\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,(E(x,y)\wedge H(y))\wedge\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,(E(y,x)\wedge H(y))\big)

\displaystyle\land\,(V(x)\vee H(x))\rightarrow

\displaystyle\big(\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,E(x,y)\land\neg\exists^{\geq 2}y\,E(y,x)\big)

4.

The borders are consistent:

\displaystyle\forall y_{1}y_{2}\,\Big(

\displaystyle\big((E(x,y_{1})\lor E(y_{1},x))\land(E(y_{1},y_{2})\lor E(y_{2},y_{1}))\land V(y_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\big((B^{\downarrow}(x)\leftrightarrow B^{\downarrow}(y_{2}))\land(B^{\uparrow}(x)\leftrightarrow B^{\uparrow}(y_{2}))\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\land\,\forall y_{1}y_{2}\,\Big(

\displaystyle\big((E(x,y_{1})\lor E(y_{1},x))\land(E(y_{1},y_{2})\lor E(y_{2},y_{1}))\land H(y_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\big((B^{\rightarrow}(x)\leftrightarrow B^{\rightarrow}(y_{2}))\land(B^{\leftarrow}(x)\leftrightarrow B^{\leftarrow}(y_{2}))\big)\Big)

5.

Every grid vertex is uniquely tiled:

G(x)\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in T}\Big(P_{t}(x)\wedge\bigwedge_{t^{\prime}\in T\setminus\{t\}}\neg P_{t^{\prime}}(x)\Big)

6.

The matching conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle(G(x)

\displaystyle\wedge\neg B^{\uparrow}(x))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bigvee_{(t,t^{\prime})\in V}\Big(P_{t}(x)\wedge\exists y_{1}\,\big(E(x,y_{1})\wedge V(y_{1})\wedge\exists y_{2}\,(E(y_{1},y_{2})\wedge P_{t^{\prime}}(y_{2})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\wedge\ (G(x)

\displaystyle\wedge\neg B^{\rightarrow}(x))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bigvee_{(t,t^{\prime})\in H}\Big(P_{t}(x)\wedge\exists y_{1}\,\big(E(x,y_{1})\wedge H(y_{1})\wedge\exists y_{2}\,(E(y_{1},y_{2})\wedge P_{t^{\prime}}(y_{2})\big)\Big)

7.

The borders are tiled as required:

\displaystyle(B^{\leftarrow}(x)\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in L}P_{t}(x))\ \wedge\ (B^{\rightarrow}(x)\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in R}P_{t}(x))

\displaystyle\wedge\

\displaystyle(B^{\downarrow}(x)\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in D}P_{t}(x))\ \wedge\ (B^{\uparrow}(x)\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in U}P_{t}(x)).

It is routine to verify that
\varphi_{S}
is a GHML
-
formula and that it satisfies Condition (a).
We argue that it satisfies Condition (b) as well.

Given a model
\mathfrak{A}=(A,G^{\mathfrak{A}},V^{\mathfrak{A}},H^{\mathfrak{A}},{B^{\leftarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}},{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}},{B^{\uparrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}},{B^{\downarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}},E^{\mathfrak{A}})
we first construct an intermediate partial function
g:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to G^{\mathfrak{A}}
such that all defined
g(i,j),g(i+1,j)
are connected with a common vertex
v
that satisfies
H(v)
, all defined
g(i,j),g(i,j+1)
are connected with a common vertex
v
that satisfies
V(v)
, and such that borders are consistent.
Given this function
g
we can then construct a solution
f
for
S
.

We construct
g
inductively, starting with the lower border, and then defining more rows moving upwards.
The lower border is also defined inductively, and we first set
g(1,1)
to a value that satisfies
B^{\rightarrow}(\alpha)\land B^{\downarrow}(\alpha)\land G(\alpha)
.

If
g(1,1),\ldots,g(k,1)
are defined, there are now two cases to consider

•

If
g(k,1)\in{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
, then the lower border is finished.

•

If
g(k,1)\not\in{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
, then by Formula
1
, there exists an element
a\in G^{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
g(k,1)
has a
H^{\mathfrak{A}}
successor, of which
a
is a successor.
We set
g(k+1,1)
to
a
.
These formulas also require
a\in G^{\mathfrak{A}}
.
The Formulas
1
and
4
require that
a\not\in{B^{\leftarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
and
a\in{B^{\downarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
.
Thus, the borders stay consistent.

The first case has to be satisfied after finitely many steps. Assume the contrary, then after at most
|A|+1
iterations there exist
i<j
such that
g(i,1)=g(j,1)
.
W.l.o.g. this is the only pair of indices that satisfy this equality.
Since
g(j,1)
has an
H
-predecessor,
g(i,1)\not\in{B^{\leftarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
must hold, thus
i\neq 1
and both
g(i-1,1)
and
g(j-1,1)
are defined and they satisfy
g(i-1,1)\neq g(j-1,1)
.
Therefore,
g(j,1)
has two
H
-predecessors or its
H
-predecessor has two
G
predecessors, which contradicts Formula
3
.
Hence, the first case is satisfied after finitely many steps and the first row is well-defined.

For the next rows, assume
g(i,j)
is defined for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
.
Then there are again two cases:

•

If there exists an
i
such that
g(i,m)\in{B^{\uparrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
, then by Formula
4
, all
g(j,m)
are in
{B^{\uparrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
.
We can thus stop, and proceed with the definition of the solution
f
.
This case has to be satisfied eventually, which can be shown by using Formula
3
with a similar argument as before.

•

Otherwise,
g(1,m)
has a
V
successor, which has a
G
-successor which we set as
g(1,m+1)
. It satisfies
g(1,m+1)\in{B^{\leftarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
,
g(1,m+1)\not\in{B^{\downarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
and
g(1,m+1)\in{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}\iff g(1,m)\in{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
by Formula
4
.

For
1\leq i<n
, let
g(i+1,m+1)
be the vertex
v
such that
g(i,m+1)
has an
H
successor that has
v
as a successor and such that
g(i+1,m)
has a
V
successor that has
v
as a successor.
This vertex exists because of Formula
2
.
By Formulas
1
and
4
,
v\not\in{B^{\leftarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
,
v\not\in{B^{\downarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
and
v\in{B^{\rightarrow}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
iff
i+1=n
.

Using the function
g
, it is easy to construct a solution
f
for
S
.
Formula
5
can be used to define
f
, while formulas
6
and
7
ensure that
f
is a valid solution.

To get rid of the unary atoms, we replace
P_{k}(x)
with the formula

\displaystyle\exists yy_{1}\cdots y_{\ell}z\,\big(E(y_{k},y_{k})\land E(x,y)\land E(y,y)\land E(y,y_{1})\land\bigwedge E(y_{i},y_{i+1})\land E(y_{\ell},z)

\displaystyle\land\neg\exists z^{\prime}\,E(z,z^{\prime})\land\bigwedge E(y_{i},y_{i})\rightarrow\exists^{\geq 2}y_{i}^{\prime}\,E(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\big),

where
\ell
is the number of unary relation symbols.

Since
\varphi_{S}
does not require any self-loops, they can be used safely to indicate unary facts.
The variable
y
is used to not confuse the facts of neighboring vertices, and
z
can be used to construct models in which all
y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}
are forced to bind to different vertices.
It is easy to adapt the proof above to this setting, although the degree is now bounded by
5
instead of
4
.

For the connected but unbounded case, we use the correspondence between connected GHML
-
and connected
\mathsf{sum}
-DHNs.
We will adapt the above formula such that it can be translated into a connected GHML
-
formula.
For this we have to introduce a free variable, which we achieve by removing the first existential quantifier in
\varphi_{S}
:

\varphi_{S}^{\prime}(\alpha)=B^{\leftarrow}(\alpha)\land B^{\downarrow}(\alpha)\land G(\alpha)\land\forall z\,\vartheta(z).

Since removing outermost existential quantifiers does not change satisfiability of first-order formulas,
\varphi_{S}^{\prime}
is satisfiable iff
\varphi_{S}
is.
The universal quantifier is still unconnected to
\alpha
, hence we use
\alpha
as a ”spypoint” and introduce edges from
\alpha
to every grid vertex with an intermediate vertex that satisfies a new unary relation
C
.
We thus adapt
\varphi_{S}^{\prime}
as follows:

•

The free variable
\alpha
has to be connected to itself, therefore we add the conjunct

\exists y\,(E(\alpha,y)\land C(y)\land E(y,\alpha))
.

•

We universally quantify all vertices connected to
\alpha
:
\forall yx\,((E(\alpha,y)\land C(y)\land E(y,x))\rightarrow\vartheta(x))
.
This formula is equivalent to

\neg\exists yx\,(E(\alpha,y)\land C(y)\land E(y,x)\land\neg\vartheta(x)),

and is thus still in GHML
-
.

•

Since
\vartheta
is negated in the formula above, we cannot introduce
\alpha
as a new free variable.
To rediscover
\alpha
we first require that each grid vertex has at most one connected vertex that is in the bottom left. We add the following conjunct to
\vartheta
:

\neg\exists^{\geq 2}yz\,(E(y,x)\land C(y)\land E(z,y)\land B^{\downarrow}(z)\land B^{\leftarrow}(z)\land G(z))

•

We can now adapt Formula
2
such that the newly introduced grid vertex also has a connection to a vertex in the bottom left.
In particular we can require that originating and the new vertex have a common neighbor in the bottom left, which by the previous item is unique.
We add to Formula
2
the following conjunct to the right side:

\displaystyle\exists c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\,(

\displaystyle B^{\leftarrow}(c_{1})\land B^{\downarrow}(c_{1})\land G(c_{1})

\displaystyle\land E(c_{1},c_{2})\land C(c_{2})\land E(c_{2},x)\land E(c_{1},c_{3})\land C(c_{3})\land E(c_{3},u)).

•

Analogously, in Formula
1
we add to the right side:

\displaystyle\exists c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\,(

\displaystyle B^{\leftarrow}(c_{1})\land B^{\downarrow}(c_{1})\land G(c_{1})

\displaystyle\land E(c_{1},c_{2})\land C(c_{2})\land E(c_{2},x)\land E(c_{1},c_{3})\land C(c_{3})\land E(c_{3},y_{2})).

It is easy to verify that this formula is satisfiable iff
\varphi_{S}
is satisfiable, and that this formula can be translated into a connected GHML
-
formula.
Since connected GHML
-
formulas can be transformed into connected DHNs, this proves the undecidability of connected DHNs in the unbounded degree case.
∎

B.4
Mean Aggregation

We first give a more thorough introduction to RHML.

RHML is an extension of HML since existential quantifiers can be expressed in RHML as follows:
let
\varphi=\exists\bar{y}\,(\psi_{1}(x,\bar{y})\land\psi_{2}(x,\bar{y}))
be an HML formula where
\psi_{1}
is a conjunction of relational atoms and
\psi_{2}
is a conjunction of unary HML formulas.
Then
\varphi
is equivalent to

\exists_{>0}\,\bar{y}\,\big(\psi_{2}(x,\bar{y}),\psi_{1}(x,\bar{y})\big).

Since
\exists_{>0}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu,\nu)
corresponds to existential quantifiers of HML, we define that
\exists_{>t}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu,\nu)
is not satisfied if there are no values that satisfy
\nu
.
Analogously, since
\exists_{\geq 1}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu,\nu)
corresponds to the negation of existential quantifiers, we define that
\exists_{\geq t}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu,\nu)
is satisfied if there are no values that satisfy
\nu
.

As a useful abbreviation, we define

\displaystyle\exists_{=t}\,\bar{y}

\displaystyle\,(\mu(x,\bar{y}),\nu(x,\bar{y}))=\exists_{\geq t}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu(x,\bar{y}),\nu(x,\bar{y}))\land\neg\exists_{>t}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu(x,\bar{y}),\nu(x,\bar{y})).

Notice that
\exists_{=t}\,\bar{y}(\,\mu,\nu)
is also satisfied if there are no values that satisfy
\nu
.

An RHML formula
\varphi
is
connected
if for every subformula
\exists_{\sim t}\,\bar{y}\,(\mu(x,\bar{y}),\nu(x,\bar{y}))
of
\varphi
, the database induced by
\nu
is connected and uses
x
.

We now show how RHML and
\mathsf{mean}
-DHNs correspond to each other.
We start with translating RHML formulas into
\mathsf{mean}
-DHNs.
The following lemma proves Point 1 and one of the directions in Point 2 of Theorem
10
.

Lemma 39
.

1.

Every (connected) RHML formula is equivalent to a (connected)
\mathsf{mean}
-DHN;

2.

let
B\geq 0
;
on databases of degree at most
B
, every connected RHML formula is equivalent to a connected simple
\mathsf{mean}
-DHN.

Proof.

We first describe the parts that both points have in common.
They will only differ in their combination function for the translation of quantifiers.
Let
\varphi
be an RHML formula and let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}
be an enumeration of the subformulas of
\varphi
such that if
\varphi_{\ell}
is a subformula of
\varphi_{k}
, then
\ell\leq k
.
In analogy with Lemma
24
, we will construct a
\mathsf{mean}
-DHN
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
, with each layer of output dimension
k
.

We encode satisfaction of
\varphi_{i}
by a
1
in the
i
-th component of the feature vectors, and we store a
0
if
\varphi_{i}
is not satisfied.
The DHN will evaluate the
i
-th subformula in the
i
-th layer, that is, after
\mathcal{L}_{i}
all subformulas
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{i}
are encoded correctly.

We have seen in Lemma
24
that DHNs can realize negations and disjunctions.
For these operators, we only used homomorphism queries with one value and without any facts, thus we can use
\mathsf{mean}
instead of
\mathsf{max}
.

For the translation of quantifiers, let
\varphi_{\ell}(y_{0})=\exists_{\sim t}\,y_{1}\cdots y_{n}\,(\mu(\bar{y}),\nu(\bar{y}))
, where
{\sim\,\in\{>,\geq\}}
, and let
\mu=\mu_{0}(y_{0})\land\cdots\land\mu_{n}(y_{n})
, where each
\mu_{i}
is a conjunction of unary formulas.

By assumption,
\nu
defines a database
G_{\ell}^{y_{0}}
, which is connected if
\nu(\bar{y})
is connected.

The layer
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}
uses the following homomorphism queries:

•

(G_{\ell}^{y_{0}},\xi,\mathsf{mean})
, where
\xi_{i}(\bar{x})
returns 1 if in
\bar{x}
all entries that correspond to conjuncts in
\mu_{i}
are set to 1, and returns 0 otherwise.

•

(G_{\ell}^{y_{0}},\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{mean})
, where
\xi_{i}^{\prime}(\bar{x})
always returns 1.

•

(F^{\bullet},\chi,\mathsf{mean})
, where
F^{\bullet}
is the database with one value and without any facts, and where
\chi(\bar{x})=\bar{x}
if
\ell\geq 1
, and
\chi(\bar{x})
returns the vector with all entries set to
0
otherwise.

The combination function
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}(x_{1},x_{2},\bar{x}_{3})
does the following:
if
x_{2}
is
0
, which only happens if there are no homomorphisms from
G_{\ell}^{y_{0}}
to the input database, and
\sim
is
\geq
, then
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}
replaces the
\ell
-th entry in
\bar{x}_{3}
with
1
.
If
x_{2}
is
0
and
\sim
is
>
, then
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}
replaces the
\ell
-th entry in
\bar{x}_{3}
with
0
.
Otherwise,
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}
replaces the
\ell
-th position in
\bar{x}_{3}
by
1
if
x_{1}\sim t
and by
0
otherwise.
In all cases it does not return
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

In the general setting of Point 1 this function is clearly non-continuous at
(t,1,\bar{x}_{3})
for each
\bar{x}_{3}
.
In the restricted settings of Point 2, there are only finitely many possible input values for
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}
.

x_{1}
is a rational number and its denominator is bounded by the number of homomorphisms, which itself is bounded because in this case
G_{\ell}^{y_{0}}
is connected and we only consider databases of degree at most
B
.
Additionally,
x_{2}
and each entry of
\bar{x}_{3}
is either
0
or
1
.
It is well known that FNN with
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
can realize functions with finitely many input values.

For both points we use the same classification function, namely

\mathsf{cls}(\bar{x})=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }x_{k}\geq 1\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is straightforward to show the correctness of the translation, that is:

Claim.
For all databases
G
, values
v\in V(G)
, and
i,j

with
1\leq i\leq j\leq k
, the following holds:

(\lambda^{j}_{\mathcal{N},G}(v))_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }G\models\varphi_{i}(v),\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

∎

We now show the second direction of Point 2 in Theorem
10
.

Lemma 40
.

Let
B\geq 0
. On databases of degree at most
B
, every connected
\mathsf{mean}
-DHN is equivalent to a connected RHML formula.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
be a
\mathsf{mean}
-DHN and let
d
be the largest diameter of a homomorphism query in
\mathcal{N}
.
Then for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, the set

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell}=\{\lambda_{\mathcal{N},G}^{\ell}(v)\mid\

\displaystyle G\text{ is a database of degree at most }B,~v\in\mathsf{adom}(G),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\text{there is no value of distance from $v$ exceeding }d\ell\}

is finite because there are only finitely many databases that satisfy these conditions.

It is easy to see that

\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell}=\{\lambda_{\mathcal{N},G}^{\ell}(v)\mid G\text{ is a database of degree at most }B,v\in\mathsf{adom}(G)\},

since
\lambda_{\mathcal{N},G}^{\ell}(v)
clearly only depends on the
\ell d
-neighborhood of
v
.

For each
\bar{x}\in\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell}
we can now proceed to construct a formula
\psi_{\bar{x}}^{\ell}(y)
such that

G\models\psi_{\bar{x}}^{\ell}(v)\text{ iff }\lambda_{\mathcal{N},G}^{\ell}(v)=\bar{x}\text{ for all databases }(G,v)\text{ of bounded degree }B.

The construction is by induction on
\ell
.
Since the input dimension of DHNs is
0
,
\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{0}
only contains the empty vector and
\psi^{0}_{()}
can be expressed by any tautology.
For the induction step, let
\ell>0
and let
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}=((F_{1}^{\bullet},\mu_{1},\mathsf{mean}),\ldots,(F_{m}^{\bullet},\mu_{m},\mathsf{mean}),\mathsf{com}_{\ell})
.
Then every vector
\bar{x}\in\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell}
can be represented as
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}(\bar{y}_{1},\ldots,\bar{y}_{m})
where each
\bar{y}_{i}
is the result of the homomorphism query
(F_{i}^{\bullet},\mu_{i},\mathsf{mean})
.
For
1\leq i\leq m
, the computation of
\bar{y}_{i}
can be described as follows:
there exists a mapping
r_{i}
that describes the distribution of homomorphisms and labelings that results in
\bar{y}_{i}
. More precisely,
r_{i}
takes the form

r_{i}:\left(\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell-1}\right)^{V(F_{i})}\to\left\{\frac{i}{j}\mid 0\leq i\leq j\leq{((B+1)^{d+1})}^{|V(F_{i})|}\right\}

and satisfies
\sum_{\lambda}r_{i}(\lambda)=1
or
\sum_{\lambda}r_{i}(\lambda)=0
and

\bar{y}_{i}=\sum_{\lambda\in\left(\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell-1}\right)^{V(F_{i})}}\Big(r_{i}(\lambda)\cdot\prod_{v\in V(F_{i})}\mu_{v}(\lambda(v))\Big).

Note that we use
\sum_{\lambda}r_{i}(\lambda)=0
to describe the case that there are no homomorphisms from
F_{i}^{\bullet}
to
G^{v}
, which results in
\bar{y}_{i}=\bar{0}
.

The restriction to mappings
r_{i}
with range
\{\frac{i}{j}\mid 0\leq i\leq j\leq{((B+1)^{d+1})}^{|V(F_{i})|}\}
suffices, since we only consider databases of bounded degree
B
and connected homomorphism queries.
That is, the
d
-neighborhood of each value contains at most
\sum_{i=0}^{d}B^{i}\leq(B+1)^{d+1}
values and there are at most
{((B+1)^{d+1})}^{|V(F_{i})|}
homomorphisms from
F_{i}
to such a neighborhood.

Next we will define an RHML formula which is satisfied by
G^{v}
iff the result of the homomorphism query
(F^{v}_{i},\mu_{i},\mathsf{mean})
is
\bar{y}_{i}
.
To this end, let
V(F^{v_{0}})=\{v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and let
R_{\bar{y}_{i}}
be the set of all functions
r_{i}
that describe a distribution of homomorphisms and labelings that result in
\bar{y}_{i}
.
Let
\nu
be the conjunction of atoms which define
F^{v}_{i}
.
To handle the edge case where there is no homomorphism from
F_{i}^{v}
, we define
\zeta_{r_{i}}
as

\zeta_{r_{i}}=\exists_{>0}\,x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\,(\top,\nu(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})),

if
\sum_{\lambda}r_{i}(\lambda)=1
and
\zeta_{r_{i}}=\top
otherwise, where
\top
is any tautology.
We can now define a formula that checks whether the embedding in the
\ell
-th layer for a given value is
\bar{y}_{i}
:

\xi_{\bar{y}_{i}}^{\ell}(x_{0})=\bigvee_{r\in R_{\bar{y}_{i}}}\bigg(\zeta_{r}\,\land\bigwedge_{\lambda\in\left(\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell-1}\right)^{V(F_{i})}}\exists_{=r(\lambda)}\,x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\,\Big(\bigwedge_{i=0}^{n}\psi_{{\lambda({v}_{i})}}^{\ell-1}(x_{i}),\nu(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\Big)\bigg).

For each
1\leq i\leq m
let
Y_{i}
be the set of all possible vectors
\bar{y}_{i}
. This set is again finite, since there are only finitely many functions
r_{i}
.
We can thus define
\psi_{\bar{x}}^{\ell}
as follows:

\psi_{\bar{x}}^{\ell}=\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{x}=\mathsf{com}_{\ell}(\bar{y}_{1},\ldots,\bar{y}_{m})\\
\bar{y}_{i}\in Y_{i}\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{\bar{y}_{i},i}^{\ell}.

Since the disjunction enumerates all reachable inputs to
\mathsf{com}_{\ell}
that result in
\bar{x}
, the formula
\psi_{\bar{x}}^{\ell}
is satisfied by
G^{v}
iff
\lambda_{\mathcal{N},G}^{\ell}(v)=\bar{x}
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4

We start with a more in-depth introduction to UQAFO. We say that a UQAFO formula is

of the existential kind
if
it can be generated by the

\varphi_{\exists}(\bar{x})
production, and likewise for formulas of the
universal kind
and
\varphi_{\forall}(\bar{x})
.

Example 41
.

The formula
\psi(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})=\exists z\,(E(x_{1},z)\land E(z,x_{2}))\land\forall z\,(E(y_{1},z)\land E(z,y_{2}))
is a UQAFO formula, but neither
of the existential kind nor of the universal
kind. In contrast,
\exists x\exists y\,(\forall z\,R(x,z)\land\forall z\,S(y,z))
is a UQAFO formula of the existential kind.

A straightforward analysis of the defining grammar shows that for every UQAFO formula
\varphi(\bar{x})
of the existential kind, the negation normal form of
\neg\varphi(\bar{x})
is a UQAFO formula of the universal kind, and vice versa. We thus obtain the following basic observation.

Lemma 42
.

For every UQAFO formula
\varphi(\bar{x})
of the existential kind, there is a UQAFO formula
\psi(\bar{x})
of the universal kind such that
\psi(\bar{x})\equiv\neg\varphi(\bar{x})
, and vice versa.

See
11

Proof.

The main step towards proving that UQAFO is at least as expressive as UNFOC is to show that for each GHML formula there is an equivalent UQAFO formula that is both of the existential and of the universal kind. We do this by structural induction.
It is easy to see that this class of UQAFO formulas is closed under disjunction. By
Lemma
42
, it is also closed under negation. It thus
remains to treat formulas
\exists^{\geq n_{1}}y_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq n_{k}}y_{k}\,\bigvee_{i}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
,
which constitute the induction start if each
\psi_{i}
contains only
atomic formulas, but which also occur in the induction step. We shall not distinguish between these two kinds of occurrences.

First note that each conjunction
\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is equivalent to
a UQAFO formula of the existential kind, by induction hypothesis and since formulas of the existential kind are closed under conjunction.
Since they are also closed under disjunction,

\bigvee_{i}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is also a UQAFO formula of the existential kind.
The counting quantifiers can
be rewritten using existential quantifiers and inequalities.
The resulting formula is of the existential kind and since there is only one free variable, it is also of the universal kind.

By Lemma
6
and what we have just shown, every UNFOC formula in one free variable is equivalent to a UQAFO formula. Note that every UNFOC formula, independently of the number of free variables, can be written as a combination in terms of
\wedge
and
\vee
of the following: atoms
R(\bar{x})
,
formulas
\exists^{\geq n}x\,\varphi
, and formulas
\neg\varphi(x)
. It is easy to see that every such combination is equivalent to an existential UQAFO formula. In particular,

\exists^{\geq n}x\,\varphi
can be rewritten
using existential quantifiers and inequalities
and for formulas
\neg\varphi(x)
we use that every UNFOC formula in one free variable is equivalent to a UQAFO formula.

It remains to show that UQAFO is strictly more expressive than GHML. This is the case since the UQAFO formula

\forall yz\,((E(x,y)\land E(y,z))\rightarrow E(x,z))

expresses local transitivity which by Lemma
35
cannot be expressed in GHML.
∎

We next argue that EML does not become more expressive when counting quantifiers are added.
Tuple counting quantifiers of the form
\exists^{\geq n}\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
are the most straightforward to express in EML.
They can be written as

\exists\bar{y}_{1}\cdots\bar{y}_{n}\,\big(\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\psi(x,\bar{y}_{i})\land\bigwedge_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\bar{y}_{i}\neq\bar{y}_{j}\big),

where
\bar{y}_{i}\neq\bar{y}_{j}
is an abbreviation for
\bigvee_{1\leq k\leq|\bar{y}|}(\bar{y}_{i})_{k}\neq(\bar{y}_{j})_{k}
.
Using distributivity laws, the disjunctions can be moved in front of the existential quantifiers.
The resulting formula is then an EML formula.
By applying this approach inductively, we can express counting quantifiers of the form
\exists^{\geq n_{1}}\bar{y}_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq n_{k}}\bar{y}_{k}\,\psi(x,\bar{y}_{1},\ldots,\bar{y}_{k})
as well:
We can replace the outermost counting quantifier in
\exists x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}\exists^{\geq n_{1}}\bar{y}_{1}\cdots\exists^{\geq n_{k}}\bar{y}_{k}\,\psi(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell},\bar{y}_{1},\ldots,\bar{y}_{k})
by introducing
n_{1}
copies of the variables
\bar{y}_{1}
and we get the equivalent formula

\exists x_{1}\cdots x_{\ell}\,\bar{y}_{1}^{1}\cdots\bar{y}_{1}^{n_{1}}\!\!\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\!\!\big(\exists^{\geq n_{2}}\bar{y}_{2}\cdots\exists^{\geq n_{k}}\bar{y}_{k}\,\psi(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell},\bar{y}_{1}^{i},\bar{y}_{2},\ldots,\bar{y}_{k})\land\!\!\bigwedge_{1\leq i<j\leq n_{1}}\!\!\bar{y}_{1}^{i}\neq\bar{y}_{1}^{j}\big).

By using variable renaming and scope changes, we can move the conjunction between existential quantifiers inwards behind all existential quantifiers.
We can deal with the disjunctions introduced by
\bar{y}_{1}^{i}\neq\bar{y}_{1}^{j}
as before.

C.1
Point 1 of Theorem
12

We prove Point 1 of
Theorem
12

and the inclusion part of Point 2. To start with, note that, trivially,
every EML formula is a UQAFO formula.

Lemma 43
.

Every unary UQAFO formula is equivalent to an EML formula.

Proof.

To transform a unary UQAFO formula
\varphi
into an EML formula, we group the quantifiers first, and then we replace universal quantifiers with negated existential quantifiers.
After grouping the quantifiers each subformula
\psi(\bar{x})=\exists y\,\psi^{\prime}(\bar{x},y)
of
\varphi
should satisfy:

•

\psi^{\prime}
is of the form
\exists z\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(\bar{x},y,z)
, or

•

\psi^{\prime}
is a Boolean combination of relational atoms, (in-)equalities, and unary UQAFO formulas.

Analogous conditions should be satisfied by subformulas
\forall y\,\psi^{\prime}(\bar{x},y)
.

The translation of UQAFO formulas into one that satisfies these conditions is by induction on the quantifier depth.
For formulas of quantifier depth
0
there is nothing to do.
For the induction step, we describe how to translate formulas that start with an existential quantifier.
The case for formulas that start with a universal quantifier is analogous.
Let
\psi(\bar{x})=\exists y\,\psi^{\prime}(\bar{x},y)
be a formula of quantifier depth
i+1
that does not satisfy any of the above conditions.
We can write
\psi^{\prime}
as a Boolean combination of relational atoms, (in-)equalities, and UQAFO formulas which
start immediately with a quantifier
.
Let
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{n}
be the relational atoms, (in-)equalities and unary UQAFO formulas, and let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
be the remaining non-unary UQAFO formulas of this Boolean combination.
Since
\psi
is a UQAFO formula, all
\xi_{j}
have to start with an existential quantifier.
By induction hypothesis, all of
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
can be transformed such that they satisfy the first condition above, in particular, they can be written as
\xi_{j}\equiv\exists\bar{y}_{j}\,\xi_{j}^{\prime}
, where
\xi_{j}^{\prime}
does not start with an existential quantifier.
By variable renaming, we can assume that all
\bar{y}_{i}
as well as
\bar{x}
and
y
are pairwise disjoint.
It is thus safe to move all quantifiers to the front, and therefore
\psi
is equivalent to

\exists y\ \bar{y}_{1}\cdots\bar{y}_{k}\,\psi^{\prime\prime},

where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is the result of replacing every occurrence
\xi_{i}
with
\xi_{i}^{\prime}
in
\psi^{\prime}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

\psi^{\prime\prime}
is then a Boolean combination of
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{n}
as well as the atoms, (in-)equalities and unary UQAFO formulas in
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}
.
Hence,
\psi
and its subformulas satisfy the conditions above.

We can now get rid of all universal quantifiers, by replacing each
\forall\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
, where
\psi
is a Boolean combination of relational atoms, (in-)equalities and unary UQAFO formulas with

\neg\exists\bar{y}\,\neg\psi(x,\bar{y}).

The negation in front of the existential quantifier is allowed in EML since it negates a unary formula.
This step also replaces every universal quantifier, since the formula satisfies the conditions above.
By using well-known logical equivalences, we can move the negation in front of
\psi
inwards, until it is in front of the atoms, (in-)equalities and unary UQAFO formulas.

In the last step, for each formula
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
, we transform
\psi(x,\bar{y})
in disjunctive normal form, move the disjunctions in front of the existential quantifiers and use variable renaming to get rid of the remaining equalities.
∎

We next show that every EML formula can be converted into a certain strict form. In a formula in this form, every subformula
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
, describes a proper embedding, rather than only a ‘partial’ embedding.
In subsequent constructions, it will be useful to assume that EML formulas are in strict form.

We say that an EML formula
\varphi
is in
strict form
if for each subformula
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
,
\psi
contains at least the following conjuncts:

•

every inequality
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
for every pair of variables
z_{1},z_{2}\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}
with
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
.

•

every relational atom
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
or its negation
\neg R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
, for each
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}
and each relational symbol in the schema.

Lemma 44
.

For each EML formula there exists an equivalent strict EML formula.

Proof.

We show by induction on the structure of EML formulas how to transform an EML formula into a strict one.
We only have to show in the induction step how to transform existential quantifiers.
Let
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
be an EML formula.
Then
\psi(x,\bar{y})
can be written as
\psi_{1}(x,\bar{y})\land\psi_{2}(x,\bar{y})
, where
\psi_{1}
is a conjunction of relational atoms, negated relational atom and inequalities, while
\psi_{2}
is a conjunction of unary EML formulas.
By the induction hypothesis, each conjunct in
\psi_{2}
can be translated into a strict EML formula, thus
\psi_{2}
is equivalent to a conjunction of strict unary EML formulas.
We denote this formula by
\psi_{2}^{\prime}
.

\psi_{1}
is equivalent to a disjunction
\bigvee_{i}\varphi_{i}(x,\bar{y})
where each
\varphi_{i}
is a conjunction such that:

•

For each relational atom
R
of arity
k
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}
it either contains
R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
or
\neg R(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
as a conjunct.

•

For each pair of variables
z_{1},z_{2}\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}
, it contains either
z_{1}=z_{2}
or
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
as a conjunct.

The formula
\exists\bar{y}\,\psi(x,\bar{y})
is then equivalent to

\bigvee_{i}\exists\bar{y}\,(\varphi_{i}(x,\bar{y})\land\psi_{2}^{\prime}(x,\bar{y}).

Each equivalence can be removed by variable renaming.
The resulting formula is then a strict EML formula.
∎

We next show that max-DENs can realize every strict EML formula and thus also every unary UQAFO formula.

Lemma 45
.

Every strict EML formula
is equivalent to a max-DEN.

Proof.

This proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
24
, that is, we translate each strict EML formula to a
\mathsf{max}
-DEN.
In particular, we use the definition of
subformula
according to the syntax rules of EML.
Let
\varphi
be a strict EML formula and let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}
be an enumeration of the subformulas in
\varphi
such that if
\varphi_{\ell}
is a subformula of
\varphi_{k}
then
\ell\leq k
.

We construct a
\mathsf{max}
-DEN
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
with
k
layers, all of output dimension
k
.
As usual, we encode satisfaction of
\varphi_{i}
with
1
and falsification with
0
in the
i
-th component.
The DEN evaluates one subformula in each layer so that for every
1\leq i\leq k
, from layer
i
on all subformulas
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{i}
are encoded correctly.

For formulas
\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{i_{1}}\lor\varphi_{i_{2}}
and
\varphi_{i}=\neg\varphi_{j}
we can adapt the homomorphism queries in the proof of Lemma
24
.
There we used exactly one homomorphism query
(F^{\bullet},\mu,\mathsf{max})
, where
F^{\bullet}
was a single-value database without any facts and where
\mathsf{com}
was an affine function followed by
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
.
For
\mathsf{max}
-DENs we cannot use
F^{\bullet}
as the only database, but we now must use multiple embedding queries to exactly match the relational atoms satisfied at the values in the input database.
We thus use
(F^{\bullet}_{1},\mu,\mathsf{max}),\ldots,(F^{\bullet}_{\ell},\mu,\mathsf{max})
, where
F^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,F^{\bullet}_{\ell}
is an enumeration of all single-value databases over the schema
\mathbf{S}
.
We replace the combination function by
\mathsf{com}^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{1},\ldots,\bar{x}_{\ell})=\mathsf{com}(\bar{x}_{1}+\cdots+\bar{x}_{\ell})
.
Since addition is affine,
\mathsf{com}^{\prime}
is still an affine function followed by
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
activation.
Additionally, the embedding query will return the same value as the homomorphism query, since exactly one
F^{\bullet}_{i}
can embed at each value.
This translation allows us to reuse the homomorphism queries which we used in Lemma
24
to encode negations and disjunctions.

For the last case, assume that
\varphi_{i}
takes the form
\exists\bar{y}\,\varphi_{k}(x,\bar{y})
, which is equivalent to the form

\exists\bar{y}\,\Big(\psi_{0}\land\bigwedge_{z\in\{x\}\cup\bar{y}}\psi_{z}\Big),

where
\psi_{0}
is a conjunction of relational atoms and each
\psi_{z}
is a conjunction of (zero or more) EML formulas with the free variable
z
.
We use the following embedding query to check whether
\varphi_{i}
itself is satisfied:

•

F_{1}^{x}
is the database that is described by
\psi_{0}
. Since
\varphi_{i}
is a strict EML formula,
F_{1}^{x}
is uniquely defined.

•

\mu_{1}
assigns to each value
z\in\mathsf{adom}(F_{1})
the transformation function that maps every tuple
\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
to the tuple
(1)
if the
j
-th position of
\bar{x}
is
1
for all conjuncts
\varphi_{j}(z)
in
\psi_{z}
, and
(0)
otherwise.
It also returns
(1)
if
\psi_{z}
is the empty conjunction.

To copy the previous feature vector, we use the embedding queries
(G^{\bullet}_{1},\mu_{2},\mathsf{max}),\ldots,(G^{\bullet}_{\ell},\mu_{2},\mathsf{max})
, where

•

G^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,G^{\bullet}_{\ell}
is an enumeration of all single-value databases over the schema
\mathbf{S}
,

•

if
i=1
,
\mu_{2}
returns the
k
-dimensional vector with all entries set to
0
,

•

if
i>1
,
\mu_{2}
is the identity function.

The combination function
\mathsf{com}_{i}
adds all vectors returned by
(G^{\bullet}_{i},\mu_{2},\mathsf{max})
, and overrides the
i
-th position in this sum by the result of the first embedding query.
It is easy to verify that all transformation functions are affine with
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
activation.

As the classification function we use

\mathsf{cls}(\bar{x})=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }x_{k}\geq 1\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is straightforward to show the correctness of the translation, that is:

Claim.
For all databases
D
, values
v\in\mathsf{adom}(G)
, and
i,j

with
1\leq i\leq j\leq k
, the following holds:

(\lambda^{j}_{\mathcal{N},G}(v))_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }G\models\varphi_{i}(v),\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

∎

Corollary 46
.

Every unary UQAFO formula is equivalent to a sum-DEN.

Proof.

To lift the previous result to sum-DENs, one can easily verify that in the previous construction all
\mu
return vectors with entries in
\{0,1\}
.
Thus each entry in the product of the generalized embedding number is in
\{0,1\}
.
In this setting for each multiset
M
over
\{0,1\}^{d}

\textup{ReLU}^{*}\left(\sum M\right)=\mathsf{max}(M).

To construct a sum-DEN, one can thus replace max with sum and apply ReLU before applying the FNN.
∎

To show that each
\mathsf{max}
-DEN can be transformed into an equivalent EML formula, we adapt the proofs of Lemma
25
and Lemma
26
.

Lemma 47
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a schema and let
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
be a
\mathsf{max}
-DEN over
\mathbf{S}
.
Then for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, the set

\chi^{\ell}_{\mathcal{N}}=\{\lambda^{\ell}_{\mathcal{N},G}(v)\mid G\text{ a $\mathbf{S}$-database and }v\in\mathsf{adom}(G)\}

is finite.

Lemma 48
.

Every
\mathsf{max}
-DEN
\mathcal{N}
is equivalent to an EML formula.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots\mathcal{L}_{k},\mathsf{cls})
be a
\mathsf{max}
-DEN.
By Lemma
47
,
we may build an equivalent EML formula

\varphi(x)
by constructing, for every

\ell\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and embedding vector
\bar{x}\in\chi^{\ell}_{\mathcal{N}}
,
an EML formula
\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}(x)
such that

G\models\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}(v)\text{ iff }\lambda^{\ell}_{\mathcal{N},G}(v)=\bar{x}\text{ for all pointed $\mathbf{S}$-databases }(G,v)

and then taking the disjunction of all
formulas
\psi^{k}_{\bar{x}}
such that
\mathsf{cls}(\bar{x})=1
.

The construction of the formulas
\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}
is by induction on
\ell
.
For
\ell=0
, the only relevant vector
\bar{x}
is the empty
vector and we may set
\psi^{0}_{()}(x)=\text{true}
.
Now let
\ell>0
and
\bar{x}\in\chi^{\ell}_{\mathcal{N}}
.
Further let
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}=(\mathcal{F}_{\ell},\mathsf{com}_{\ell})
,
with
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
the sequence
(F^{u_{1}}_{1},\mu_{1},\mathsf{max}),\dots,(F^{u_{m}},\mu_{m},\mathsf{max})
.
We define
\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}
to be a disjunction
that contains one disjunct for every possible way to choose for every

i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
a partition
X_{i},Y_{i}
of the set of mappings
\lambda:\mathsf{adom}(F_{i})\rightarrow\chi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\ell-1}
such that

\mathsf{com}_{\ell}(\bar{y}_{1},\dots,\bar{y}_{m})=\bar{x}
where, for
1\leq i\leq m
,

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{i}:=\underset{\lambda\in X_{i}}{\mathsf{max}}\prod_{v\in\mathsf{adom}(F)}\mu_{v}(\lambda(v)).

Note that the definition of
\bar{y}_{i}
corresponds to the evaluation of the embedding query
(F^{u_{i}}_{i},\mu_{i},\mathsf{max})
under any embedding
h
that is
‘compatible’ with some
\lambda\in X_{i}
, that is,
h
maps each
v\in\mathsf{adom}(F_{i})
to a vertex

h(v)
in the input graph that carries the embedding vector
\lambda(v)
. Clearly, we may view
the database
F_{i}
as a
conjunction
\vartheta_{i}
of relational
atoms and negations thereof and inequalities.
Let
\bar{z}_{i}
denote the tuple of variables in
\vartheta_{i}
, ordered arbitrarily but without
x
. Then
\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}(x)
includes as a disjunct

\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq m}\Big(\bigwedge_{\lambda\in X_{i}}\exists\bar{z}_{i}\,\big(\vartheta_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{v\in\mathsf{adom}(F_{i})}\psi^{\ell-1}_{\lambda(v)}(v)\big)\wedge\bigwedge_{\lambda\in Y_{i}}\neg\exists\bar{z}_{i}\,\big(\vartheta_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{v\in\mathsf{adom}(F_{i})}\psi^{\ell-1}_{\lambda(v)}(v)\big)\Big).

It is easy to verify that
\psi^{\ell}_{\bar{x}}
is as required.
∎

C.2
Point 2 of Theorem
12

To prove that UQAFO does not capture
\mathsf{sum}
-DENs, we use a strategy similar to the one in the proof of Proposition
8
.
That is, we first show that PQR-orders can be defined by
\mathsf{sum}
-DENs, and then introduce UQAFO games and use them to show that UQAFO cannot express PQR-orders.

Lemma 49
.

There exists a sum-DEN that accepts pointed graphs iff they are PQR-orders.

Proof.

We first use UQAFO formulas and the translation in Corollary
46
to build a
\mathsf{sum}
-DEN that checks some of these properties.
We then describe how to extend this DEN to check all requirements of a PQR-order.

•

The following UQAFO formula describes linear orders in which each vertex satisfies either
P,Q,
or
R
, and the vertex bound to the free variable satisfies
P
, and the last vertex satisfies
R
.

\displaystyle\forall yz\,\big((E(x,y)\land E(y,z))\rightarrow E(x,z)\big)

\displaystyle\land\,\forall yz\,\big((E(x,y)\land E(x,z)\land y\neq z)\rightarrow(E(y,z)\leftrightarrow\neg E(z,y))\big)

\displaystyle\land\,\forall uvw\,\big((E(x,u)\land E(u,v)\land E(v,w))\rightarrow E(u,w)\big)

\displaystyle\land\,E(x,x)\land\forall y\,\big(E(x,y)\rightarrow E(y,y)\big)

\displaystyle\land\,P(x)\land\forall y\,\big((E(x,y)\land\forall z\,(y=z\lor\neg E(y,z)))\rightarrow R(y)\big)

\displaystyle\land\,\forall y\,\big(E(x,y)\rightarrow((Q(y)\land\neg P(y)\land\neg R(y))

\displaystyle\phantom{\land\,\forall y\,\big(E(x,y)\rightarrow(}\ \lor(\neg Q(y)\land P(y)\land\neg R(y))\lor(\neg Q(y)\land\neg P(y)\land R(y)))\big)

By Corollary
46
, this can be expressed by a sum-DEN.
The first conjunct ensures that there does not exist a two-hop successor.
The second, third, and fourth conjuncts describes linear orders, that is,
E
is total, antisymmetric, transitive and reflexive.
And the last two conjuncts ensure that the free variable satisfies
P
, the last vertex satisfies
R
and every vertex satisfies either
P,Q
or
R
.

•

The usual approach in FO to express that the immediate successor satisfies
\psi
is not in UQAFO:

\varphi(x)=\exists y\,(x\neq y\land E(x,y)\land\forall z\,(x=z\lor y=z\lor\neg E(x,z)\lor\neg E(z,y))\land\psi(y))
contains a quantifier alternation with more than one free variable.
But the DEN can use its counting to check an equivalent property.
It instead computes and compares the number of
P,Q
and
R
successors.
It is easy to verify that it suffices to check the following property for all vertices
u
in the linear order:
We denote the number of successors of
u
that satisfy
P,Q
and
R
by
n_{P},n_{Q}
and
n_{R}
respectively.

–

If
u
satisfies
P
, then
n_{P}=n_{Q}=n_{R}
,

–

if
u
satisfies
Q
, then
n_{P}+1=n_{Q}=n_{R}
, and

–

if
u
satisfies
R
, then
n_{P}+1=n_{Q}+1=n_{R}
.

All these properties can be computed with a
\mathsf{sum}
-DEN with
\textup{ReLU}^{*}
activation, since
x\geq y
can be checked for
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
with
\textup{ReLU}^{*}(x-y+1)
and FNNs can realize conjunctions.

•

Last but not least, with another sum aggregation, the DEN can check for
v
that all its successors satisfy the property above.

∎

To show that this property cannot be expressed by a UQAFO formula, we
introduce Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé games for UQAFO.
We have to define the game for formulas with more than one free variable, thus we allow databases to have a tuple of distinguished values which may contain more than one entry.

Definition 50

(UQAFO game)
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
. The
\ell
-round UQAFO game is played between two players,
Spoiler
and
Duplicator
, on two pointed databases
(D_{1},v_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,v_{1}^{(n)})
and
(D_{2},v_{2}^{(1)},\ldots,v_{2}^{(n)})
.
A configuration consists of both pointed databases and additionally an index
s\in\{1,2\}
that describes which database
Spoiler
is currently playing in.
For the initial configuration
Spoiler
can choose
s\in\{1,2\}
.
In each of the
\ell
rounds,
Spoiler
can make one of the following moves:

•

Spoiler
can play a value
u_{s}
in
D_{s}
. Then
Duplicator
responds with a value
u_{3-s}
in
D_{3-s}
. The next configuration consists of the pointed databases
(D_{1},v_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,v_{1}^{(n)},u_{1})
and
(D_{2},v_{2}^{(1)},\ldots,v_{2}^{(n)},u_{2})
.
The index
s
stays unchanged.

•

Spoiler
can decide to play in
D_{3-s}
. To do so, they choose one index
1\leq k\leq n
to remember the distinguished values
v_{1}^{(k)}
and
v_{2}^{(k)}
.
Spoiler then chooses a value
u_{3-s}
in
D_{3-s}
.
Duplicator
responds with a value
u_{s}
in
D_{s}
.
The next configuration consists of the databases
(D_{1},v_{1}^{(k)},u_{1})
and
(D_{2},v_{2}^{(k)},u_{2})
and index
3-s
.

Spoiler
wins this game if for any configuration the set
\{(v_{1}^{(k)},v_{2}^{(k)})\mid 1\leq k\leq n\}
does not describe a partial isomorphism between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

Duplicator
wins if
Spoiler
does not win after
\ell
rounds.
